TREASURER: 
a. Consideration and approval of the following items: Receipt of the Annual
Report of Collections and Delinquencies in accordance with
A.R.S. §42-18002; Settlement of Accounts; and Request for Authorization
to Invest Funds Pursuant to A.R.S. §35-327(G) :
A presentation was shown.  Danielle Earl presented the 2024–2025 Tax
Collection Report, outlining the total taxes charged, collected, and still
outstanding. She indicated that after corrections, the total taxes charged
were approximately $97.1 million, with $93.96 million collected by June 30,
2025, and that as of July, the remaining balance had decreased by over
$1.2 million, leaving about 3.25% still to collect. She noted that collections
  
                                                                    2 of 16 
DRAFT

<!-- page 9 -->
$1.2 million, leaving about 3.25% still to collect. She noted that collections
are ahead of last year’s pace.  She also requested authorization, as
required by statute, to invest and reinvest county school and special district
funds for the 2025–2026 fiscal year. She indicated that although overall
investments are slightly down, interest earnings have increased by 13.4%,
with July alone showing a 94% increase over the previous year. She noted
that this is partly due to reinvestments at higher rates as older, lower-yield
investments from the COVID-19 era mature. She indicated that a sweep
account has also been implemented, ensuring all county funds are now
invested daily.  Ms. Earl reported an estimated $10,000 in anticipated
revenue from the Treasurer’s Processing Fund for uncollected taxes, based
on recent certificate sales. She expressed appreciation to her team and
various departments for their collaboration and support over the past
several months, highlighting efforts to procure new equipment and improve
department efficiency.  Supervisor Seymore inquired if the principal or
base is secured now that all accounts are being swept into an invested
fund.  Ms. Earl indicated that everything is secured, noting that everything is
in A or AAA rated funds.  Supervisor Seymore shared his appreciation for
Ms. Earl’s efforts in building a strong team, driving improvements, and
taking a proactive approach that is clearly benefiting the county.  Chairman
Whiting thanked Ms. Earl and her team for their excellent work.  He
acknowledged Ms. Earl’s recognition of others who contributed, highlighting
her good heart and the collaborative spirit within the county.
Vice-Chair Whitesinger made a motion to approve the following items:
Receipt of the Annual Report of Collections and Delinquencies in
accordance with A.R.S. §42-18002; Settlement of Accounts; and Request
for Authorization to Invest Funds Pursuant to A.R.S. §35-327(G), motion
seconded by Supervisor Seymore.  Vote unanimous approving the
motion. 
Supervisor Seymore made a motion to enter into the Board of
Equalization, motion seconded by Vice-Chair Whitesinger.  Vote
unanimous approving the motion. 
 
BOARD OF EQUALIZATION:
a. Discussion and action regarding Petition for Review of Real Property
Valuation of APN 411-52-136 (Mulligan); Swearing in of Assessor,
Appraisers and Petitioners :
Assessor, Michael Montandon, Laurie Velde and the Petitioner, Tim
Mulligan, were sworn in by Melissa Buckley, Clerk of the Board. Ms. Velde
provided a packet for the Board.  The petitioner, Tim Mulligan, addressed
the Board regarding his opposition to the proposed increase, indicating that
it appears excessive given the actual value of their property. He spoke
about data from multiple real estate sources, indicating that they all
estimate the home’s value to be significantly lower than the county's full
cash value listed on the 2026 Residential Notice of Value. He spoke about
inconsistencies in valuations compared to nearby properties, including one
directly across the street. He acknowledged a 33% increase in their home's
living space, indicating that their taxes are set to increase to more than
double, which he feels is unfair. He indicated that they are willing to pay
their fair share but urged the Board to reconsider the amount of the
proposed increase.  Ms. Velde stated that the subject property, located in
  
                                                                    3 of 16 
DRAFT

<!-- page 10 -->
Pinetop Country Club and bordering forest reservation land, underwent a
728-square-foot addition and construction of a 725-square-foot garage in
2023, increasing its livable space by roughly 33%. She indicated that these
improvements brought the total living area to 2,184 square feet and
updated the home's effective age to 1991. She noted that following
inspections in 2023, the county initially assigned the property a full cash
value of $622,129 and a limited property value (LPV) of $370,997 for 2026. 
She indicated that the property owner appealed, presenting comparable
sales data, to argue for a lower valuation, estimating the full cash value at
$502,320. She further indicated that the Assessor reviewed the appeal and
applied a $20,009 reduction to the improvement value, supported by three
sales and two equity comparisons, bringing the revised full cash value to
$602,120. Ms. Velde stated that the property’s location backing forest land
adds value and recommended that the amounts remain as proposed, noting
that the LPV is calculated in accordance with A.R.S. § 42-13301, and is not
subject to appeal.  Mr. Mulligan addressed the Board regarding an error
with the math in the report by Ms. Velde.  A discussion was held regarding
the error.  Mr. Mulligan indicated that he doesn’t fully understand the
county’s process and spoke about his observation of the property
valuations, based on Zillow and comparable sales in their neighborhood,
appearing significantly higher than what neighbors are paying per square
foot. Michael Montandon explained that Arizona’s current property tax
system, established by a 2012 proposition, creates two values: the full cash
value, estimated by the assessor, and a limited property value (LPV) set by
statute, noting that the LPV increases by 5% annually unless there’s a
major change like a home addition over 15%, which causes a larger
adjustment. He indicated that this is why the neighbor’s value is lower,
since they didn’t have a recent large addition. He indicated that while the
assessor tries to be fair with market value estimates, the LPV is legally
fixed and cannot be changed at their discretion.
Mr. Mulligan indicated that he understands the legal explanation but
expressed hope for some leniency or flexibility in the process, noting that
the average homeowner isn't familiar with real estate laws and may feel
unfairly impacted. Mr. Carlyon indicated that taxes are based on the limited
property value, which increases steadily and is set by statute, noting that
even if the market value is adjusted, it won’t affect the limited value used to
calculate taxes.  Vice-Chair Whitesinger inquired if the discrepancy in the
dollar amount would be corrected.  Mr. Montandon indicated that the
discrepancy was only on presentation and that it is correct in the system. 
Vice-Chair Whitesinger acknowledged Mr. Mulligan’s concerns,
expressed appreciation for their investment in the community, and
emphasized that the county must follow the law in these matters. 
Vice-Chair Whitesinger made a motion to uphold the Assessor’s
recommendation, motion seconded by Supervisor Seymore.  Vote
unanimous approving the motion.
Supervisor Seymore made a motion to return to regular session, motion
seconded by Supervisor Peshlakai.  Vote unanimous approving the
motion.

SHERIFF'S OFFICE:
a. Presentation of Sheriff's Commendation to Kathrine Lann for actions taken
on Friday, June 13th:
Deputy Chief Bruce Tucker spoke about an incident from June 13, 2025,
where two staff members and a citizen, Ms. Catherine Lann, stopped at a
vehicle accident before first responders arrived. He indicated that Ms. Lann
provided aid to an injured juvenile, showing care and compassion. He
expressed gratitude and formally recognized her for her selfless actions. 
Master Deputy Shane Jackson presented Ms. Lann with a certificate of
Appreciation.  Lori Mills, with Congressman Eli Crane’s office, presented
Ms. Lann with a Congressional Certificate of Recognition.  Chairman
Whiting shared his appreciation, stating the community is fortunate to have
citizens willing to step up and help in times of need.  Sheriff Clouse shared
his appreciation with Ms. Lann.
 
  
b. Presentation to Laurie Palazzolo for years of service to Navajo County
Search and Rescue (NCSAR):
Chief Deputy Brian Swanty recognized Captain Lori Palazzolo for her
exceptional 15 years of volunteer service with the Navajo County Search
and Rescue team, including 10 years as captain. He indicated that her
leadership, dedication, and impact on missions and team development
have been instrumental in saving lives and strengthening the program. He
expressed gratitude for her contributions to the county’s safety and
wellbeing.
Lori Mills, with Congressman Eli Crane’s office, presented Ms. Palazzolo
with a Congressional Certificate of Recognition.  Supervisor Seymore
shared his appreciation, noting that one of the greatest gifts is giving hope
to those in need or lost, and commended the Ms. Palazzolo for providing
that hope through her service.
 
  
c. Presentation of the Arizona Law Enforcement Accreditation Program
(ALEAP) obtained by the Navajo County Sheriff's Office from the Arizona
Association of Chiefs of Police :
Lt. Alden Whipple stated that the Navajo County Sheriff's Office has
become the first sheriff’s office in Arizona to achieve ALEAP accreditation
through the Arizona Association of Chiefs of Police.  He indicated that this
is a two-year process involved implementing rigorous standards of
professionalism, accountability, and operational excellence. He further
indicated that the achievement reflects a commitment to continuous
improvement and enhanced public trust. He noted that the office also
played a key role in adapting the accreditation framework, which was
originally designed for police departments, to fit the unique structure of
sheriff’s offices, paving the way for others in the state.  Vice-Chair
Whitesinger congratulated the Sheriff's Department for achieving
accreditation, noting that it’s a significant accomplishment for the
community. She indicated that the recognition reflects a strong commitment
to quality, proper procedures, and overall excellence in service. 
Supervisor Seymore congratulated the Sheriff's Department for their
  
                                                                    5 of 16 
DRAFT

<!-- page 12 -->
achievement in the Arizona Law Enforcement Accreditation Program
(ALEAP), indicating that it’s a significant leap forward and a standard for
other sheriff’s offices in the state.  Sheriff Clouse indicated that the
accreditation was a major two-year effort involving over 100 standards,
noting that while led by the Sheriff's Office, it also required support from
Public Works, Building and Grounds, and County Administration, including
funding and infrastructure upgrades like security cameras. He thanked all
involved for their collaboration and contribution to the successful outcome. 
Chairman Whiting expressed his appreciation for the team's efforts and
emphasized the importance of teamwork, noting that success often comes
from everyone coming together and supporting each other.
 
ADMINISTRATIVE SERVICES: 
a. Presentation to LT Dennis Warren on his Retirement from Navajo County
after 24 Years of Service:
Eric Scott recognized Lieutenant Dennis Warren for over 24 years of
dedicated service to Navajo County and the Sheriff's Office. He indicated
that Lt. Warren has consistently demonstrated professionalism and pride in
his work, particularly in maintaining a high standard at the county jail. He
stated that he is known for his positive attitude and leadership, and that he
has played a key role in making sure the jail is a clean, well-managed
facility.
Deputy Chief Garcia recognized Lt. Warren on his retirement after more
than two decades of dedicated service. He indicated that he now joins a
distinguished group of individuals who’ve left this demanding profession
with grace and a smile. He stated that he has been a joy to supervise and
will be deeply missed, noting that his legacy of excellence sets a high bar
for whoever follows in his footsteps.  Lori Mills, with Congressman Eli
Crane’s office, presented Lt. Warren with a Congressional Certificate of
Recognition. 
Mr. Scott presented Lt. Warren with a gift of appreciation for his years of
service to Navajo County. Lt. Warren thanked the Board, noting that Navajo
County was a great place to retire.  Vice-Chair Whitesinger expressed her
appreciation to Lt. Warren for his warmth, kindness, and friendly nature
over the years. She thanked him for his service to Navajo County and
wished him the best in his retirement.  Supervisor Benally thanked Lt.
Warren for his many years of dedicated service to Navajo County.  She
shared her appreciation for the time and effort he put in throughout the
years.  She wished him well in his retirement. Lt. Warren stated that he
made Navajo County his home and treated his workplace with the same
care, keeping it clean and well-maintained like his own home. He
expressed gratitude to everyone for their support and appreciation.  
Chairman Whiting shared his appreciation to Lt. Warren for always being
warm, kind, and welcoming. He indicated that his consistent
professionalism and positive presence made others feel valued and
uplifted, whether at the jail, with the Honor Guard, or elsewhere. He stated
that his genuine demeanor and the way he showed up made a lasting
impact and were deeply appreciated.  Supervisor Peshlakai thanked Lt.
Warren for his long-standing service and contributions to Navajo County,
both as an employee and in his leadership roles. He shared his
appreciation for his involvement in various activities, including recent cadet

b. Amendment to Contract with eX2 for the Design, Construction,
Maintenance and Marketing of Fiber Optic Infrastructure and an
Intergovernmental Agreement (IGA) with the City of Show Low to add the
construction of six lateral connections to the county's middle mile fiber
network :
A presentation was shown. Grant Evans stated that this is a contract
amendment with eX2 and an intergovernmental agreement (IGA) with the
City of Show Low to connect six city-owned facilities to Navajo County’s
middle mile broadband network. He indicated that the partnership allows
the city to use the county’s negotiated pricing, with the county constructing
the connections and the city reimbursing the costs. He further indicated that
the facilities include the airport, Frontier Park, public works building, city
hall, water plant, and police department, noting that, after construction, the
city will negotiate leases for certain middle mile segments separately.  He
stated that construction is planned to start late this year, following the
completion of design work. He provided an update on the overall
broadband project, including 95% of duct installation and 91% of hand
holes being complete, with fiber installation progressing behind. He
indicated that some work has been delayed due to ADOT lane closure
restrictions but will resume in October. He further indicated that the
remaining fiber installation covers key areas including Snowflake, Taylor,
Heber, Show Low, Pinetop, and Lakeside.  He recommended approval of
the contract amendment and IGA to move forward with this collaborative
project.
Vice-Chair Whitesinger shared her appreciation for the update.

for the Design, Construction, Maintenance and Marketing of Fiber Optic
Infrastructure and an Intergovernmental Agreement (IGA) with the City of
Show Low to add the construction of six lateral connections to the county's
middle mile fiber network, motion seconded by Supervisor Benally.  Vote
unanimous approving the motion. 
 
  
c. PUBLIC HEARING: TRUTH IN TAXATION pursuant to A.R.S. §42-17104
(primary) and §48-254 (secondary): Consideration and possible approval of
property tax levy with no change to property tax rates from the prior year :
A presentation was shown.  Jason Vowell spoke about the Navajo County’s
property tax rates, noting that the county is proposing to keep the primary
property tax rate flat for 2025–2026 at 0.8114. He indicated that, although
the tax rate remains unchanged, total property tax revenue will increase
due to rising property valuations and new construction. He stated that the
county operates below its maximum allowable levy and maintains one of
the lowest property tax rates in the state.  He spoke about the secondary
districts’ property tax rates, including Public Health, Flood Control, and the
  
                                                                    7 of 16

Library District, which will remain unchanged. He shared an example for an
average home in Show Low, indicating the total property tax increase is
minimal, approximately $12.75 annually, mostly due to higher property
valuations rather than rate increases.  He indicated that the county aims to
keep property taxes stable while reflecting property value changes. 
Chairman Whiting opened the meeting for public comments.  There
being no comments, the public hearing was closed. 
Vice-Chair Whitesinger made a motion to approve the property tax levy

by Supervisor Seymore.  Melissa Buckley polled the Board, with the vote

d. * PUBLIC HEARING: Convene a Special Meeting of the Navajo County
Board of Supervisors: FINAL 2024-25 BUDGET PURSUANT TO A.R.S.
§42-17104: Consideration and possible adoption of Resolution Number
23-2025, approving the proposed final budget for fiscal year 2025-26 for
Navajo County:
A presentation was shown.  Jayson Vowell stated that the proposed final
budget remains unchanged from the preliminary version presented in July.
He indicated that the county’s property tax rate will remain flat, with a
continued focus on maintaining essential services and investing in regional
projects such as broadband infrastructure, the Winslow levee, and the
Taylor medical examiner’s office. He spoke about the proposed
adjustments to employee compensation, including a 2.5% salary increase,
the introduction of sabbatical leave, and a health incentive program.  He
stated that the general fund budget is set at $68.4 million, with $50.7 million
allocated to departmental expenditures across public safety, criminal
justice, mandated services, and administration, and an additional $17.7
million is reserved for contingencies. He spoke about special revenue funds
which support specific restricted-use programs such as health services,
public works, and ARPA-funded initiatives, totaling $140.6 million.  He
provided an update on the Jail District, noting that since 2020, the district
has relied on a dedicated sales tax, which is projected to generate $6.9
million in fiscal year 2025. He indicated that fund balances have declined
due to higher expenses, especially following the county’s transition to a
third-party medical provider for inmate healthcare in 2022, noting that the
change was necessary due to insurance limitations and rising liability
concerns. He indicated that, although medical costs remain high, grant
funding from the Arizona Border Security program and general fund
transfers have helped stabilize the budget. He noted that inmate housing
revenue is expected to provide further support in the upcoming fiscal year. 
He spoke about the debt service and capital project budgets for fiscal year
2025–2026, highlighting strategic financial decisions and long-term savings.
He indicated that the debt service funds total $2.3 million, with two major
components, a 2021 pension obligation bond and a refinancing of earlier
facility improvement bonds, noting that the pension debt restructuring has
already saved $628,000 and is projected to save over $11 million by 2038
and that the facility bond refinancing has yielded $409,000 in savings so
  
                                                                    8 of 16 
DRAFT

<!-- page 15 -->
far, with total expected savings of about $769,000 through 2031.  He
indicated that the county also maintains a capital projects fund of $35.1
million, with approximately $30.5 million being allocated for the Winslow
Levee project and additional funds being designated for broadband
expansion, the Taylor medical examiner facility, code enforcement, and
regional law enforcement communications.
He stated that, in total, the county’s budget across all funds includes $68.4
million in the general fund, $140.6 million in special revenue funds, $2.3
million in debt service, and $35.1 million in capital projects, bringing the
overall fiscal year 2026 budget to $246.6 million. He noted that this reflects
a 3.3% decrease from the prior year, and future budgets are expected to
decrease further due to the phasing out of one-time funding sources like
CARES and ARPA. Chairman Whiting opened the meeting for public
comments.  There being no comments, the public hearing was closed. 
Supervisor Seymore indicated that since the jail tax was implemented, jail
district spending has increased by about $4 million, largely due to rising
medical service costs. He expressed hope for finding ways to reduce those
costs, possibly through legislative support. He thanked staff for their work
on the budget.  Vice-Chair Whitesinger expressed her appreciation for the
collaborative work on the budget, emphasizing the importance of long-term
planning to maintain quality public services. She spoke about the
complexity of the budgeting process and shared her appreciation for the
transparency and responsible use of funds. She thanked all departments
involved and highlighted the team's efforts in preparing for the future while
maintaining a flat tax rate.  Chairman Whiting thanked all county
departments and offices for their collaborative efforts during the lengthy
budget process. He acknowledged the Administration team's
responsiveness to feedback and praised fellow board members for their
serious, thoughtful engagement and commitment to understanding and
planning the budget responsibly.
Supervisor Benally made a motion to adopt Resolution Number
23-2025, approving the proposed final budget for fiscal year 2025-26 for
Navajo County, motion seconded by Vice-Chair Whitesinger.  Vote
unanimous approving the motion.

Improvement District, Silver Creek County Road Improvement
District, Victory Heights Road Maintenance District, Porter Mountain
Domestic Water Improvement District, Fawnbrook Domestic Water
Improvement District, Joseph City Street Lighting Improvement
District, Navajo County Jail District, Timberland Acres Special Road
District, White Mountain Lakes #2 Special Road District, and Silver Creek
Flood Protection District, motion seconded by Vice-Chair Whitesinger. 
Vote unanimous approving the motion.

SUPERINTENDENT OF SCHOOLS: 
a. Consideration and adoption of the Fiscal Year 2026 Tax Rates and Levies
for all Navajo County School Districts :
A presentation was shown. Rachael Lewis spoke about the property tax
levies required for each school district in Navajo County, emphasizing the
teamwork involved in calculating these rates, including contributions from
the Assessor and Treasurer. She indicated that the levies, based on
primary and secondary valuations, fund education for students across the
county. She further indicated that most school districts have voter-approved
overrides or bonds that impact rates, except a few districts on tribal lands
that are not taxed. She stated that the rates reflect factors such as
assessed property values, enrollment, transportation needs, and additional
assistance programs. She further stated that the taxes directly support
student education and highlighted the complexity behind how rates are
determined.  Chairman Whiting thanked Ms. Lewis for her leadership
and the collaborative effort in putting everything together.  Supervisor
Seymore shared his appreciation with Ms. Lewis and her team for the work
done on this.  Supervisor Peshlakai inquired if the school districts
providing transportation services have permission to go into the
unorganized territory.  Ms. Lewis indicated that the school districts use their
buses to enter the unorganized territory, which is an area not claimed by
any school district, to pick up students and that they track mileage carefully
and use a specific method to estimate transportation costs each year.
Supervisor Peshlakai inquired if Holbrook School District participates in
the program.  Ms. Lewis indicated that they currently do not, that they have
the agreement with Winslow.  
Vice-Chair Whitesinger made a motion to adopt the Fiscal Year 2026
Tax Rates and Levies for all Navajo County School Districts, motion
seconded by Supervisor Peshlakai.  Vote unanimous approving the
motion. 
 
  
BOARD OF SUPERVISORS:
a. PUBLIC HEARING: Consideration of a Liquor License for Tracy Lynn
Mulac for Bertie's 2 located at 448 Porter Mountain Rd., Lakeside, AZ:
Leah Thomas stated that a liquor license application for Birdie’s 2 for a 012
Restaurant License was submitted.  She indicated that the Treasurer’s
Office, Health Department, and Planning & Development responded that
the application meets the requirements for their departments, however, the
Sheriff’s Office opposed the application due to concerns about an individual
involved in the business who is not listed on the application and is
unqualified to hold a liquor license. She stated that the required 20-day
posting has been completed.  She requested that the Board consider all
department feedback when making a recommendation to the Department of
Liquor.  Chairman Whiting opened the meeting for public comments. 
There being no comments, the public hearing was closed.  Supervisor
Seymore inquired as to ownership of the business.  Tracy Mulac stated she
is the only owner.  Vice-Chair Whitesinger addressed concerns about a
discrepancy between the business ownership listed in the liquor license
application and the findings of the sheriff’s background check, noting that
  
                                                                    11 of 16 
DRAFT

<!-- page 18 -->
the background check revealed that there is another individual involved as
a partial owner of the business who is not listed on the liquor license
application. She stated that due to the inconsistency and the sheriff’s
concerns, she would recommend non-approval of the application, noting
that the final decision will rest with the Department of Liquor. Ms. Mulac
indicated that the partnership is for her other business, that it’s not for this
business.  Vice-Chair Whitesinger inquired of legal counsel what the
process would be.  Brad Carlyon indicated that the sheriff's investigation
revealed another business partner, who allegedly has a criminal record and
is not listed on the liquor license application, and that based on the
information the sheriff's office recommended non-approval of the license.
He further indicated that the Board of Supervisors only makes a
recommendation and that the final decision on whether to issue the liquor
license rests with the Department of Liquor Licenses. Ms. Mulac addressed
the Board regarding the information from the Sheriff’s Office. 
Vice-Chair Whitesinger made a motion to recommend denial on the
Liquor License for Tracy Lynn Mulac for Bertie's 2 located at 448 Porter
Mountain Rd., Lakeside, AZ, motion seconded by Supervisor Benally.  
Supervisor Seymore shared concerns with denying the liquor license
based solely on the sheriff’s recommendation. He indicated that the
application lists only one owner, who also claims to be the sole owner and
manager, and it's not an LLC or corporation. He further indicated that the
application meets all legal requirements, such as distance from schools or
churches. He stated that, while he respects the concerns of the sheriff's
office, he believes more evidence is needed to prove someone else is
involved in the business before recommending denial. A discussion was
held regarding the timeframe for submitting the response to the Department
of Liquor, if there would be time to table this to get additional information
regarding the sheriff’s office recommendation, and the final decision being
with the Department of Liquor.
Motion passed 4-1, with Supervisor Seymore being in opposition. 
 
b. Consideration of a Proclamation, proclaiming August 14, 2025, as "Navajo
Code Talkers Day":
Supervisor Alberto Peshlakai spoke about the proclamation to recognize
August 14, 2025, as Navajo Code Talkers Day, indicating that it honors the
service of over 400 Navajo men who were recruited by the U.S.
government to serve in military communication units during wartime. He
advised that these men, known as the Navajo Code Talkers, played a
crucial role in safeguarding national security and preserving democracy. He
indicated that the proclamation specifically acknowledges the original 29
Code Talkers who developed the code, as well as the others who served.
He requested approval of the proclamation. Vice-Chair Whitesinger 
shared her appreciation to Supervisor Peshlakai for bringing the
proclamation forward, highlighting its importance to the history of the
community.
Vice-Chair Whitesinger made a motion to approve a Proclamation,
proclaiming August 14, 2025, as "Navajo Code Talkers Day", motion
seconded by Supervisor Peshlakai.  Vote unanimous approving the
motion.

b. * Consideration of Resolution Number 24-2025 authorizing an application
                                                                    14 of 16

b. * Consideration of Resolution Number 24-2025 authorizing an application
for the AZ SMART grant in the amount of $215,000 for the construction
phase of Heber Sidewalk Improvements project:
Madhav Mundle stated that the proposed resolution is to authorize the
county's application for a SMART grant to cover the local match required for
the construction phase of the Heber Sidewalk Improvement  Project in
District 4. He indicated that the county previously received a SMART grant
for the design phase and has applied for a federal SS4A grant for
construction, noting that since SS4A requires a 20% local match, the new
SMART grant would help cover that amount. He stated that ADOT now
accepts a resolution and RFGA instead of a full IGA to expedite the
process. He stated that staff recommend approval.  Supervisor Seymore
inquired if the county does not receive the SMART grant to cover the local
match if the county would be responsible for paying that amount to proceed
with the project.  Mr. Mundle indicated that if the SMART grant isn’t
awarded, the county is not obligated to pay the match, that the requirement
is simply to apply. He further indicated that Public Works has also
proactively applied for a TA grant with a lower match rate as a backup plan.
Supervisor Seymore made a motion to adopt Resolution Number
24-2025 authorizing an application for the AZ SMART grant in the amount
of $215,000 for the construction phase of Heber Sidewalk Improvements
project, motion seconded by Supervisor Peshlakai.  Vote unanimous
approving the motion.

Submitted By: Amanda Silverhorn, Executive Secretary

Proclamation declaring September 8 through September 14, 2025, as National Patriotism week

During this event, we urge all citizens to join with the Benevolent and Protective Order of Elks in
expressing gratitude for the privilege of American Citizenship with appropriate celebrations and

Attachments
Patriotism Week, Holbrook Elks 2450 
Form Review
Inbox Reviewed By Date
Clerk of the Board Melissa Buckley 08/18/2025 10:30 AM
Form Started By: Amanda Silverhorn Started On: 08/01/2025 01:03 PM

<!-- page 24 -->
Navajo County
Proclamation
NATIONAL PATRIOTISM WEEK
WHEREAS, the Benevolent and Protective Order of Elks, through its Constitution, is a
Patriotic Order; and
WHEREAS, the Order promotes the ideals that the citizens of this nation live in freedom,
won through the great sacrifices and many tribulations which have provided the
foundation for a free, prosperous, and independent life; and
WHEREAS, we realize that each generation must work to maintain this freedom,
otherwise, through carelessness or indifference, the rights and liberties enjoyed may
vanish; and
WHEREAS, it is fitting and proper to recognize this freedom and to honor the nation,
which provides it.
NOW THEREFORE, BE IT PROCLAIMED, by the Board of Supervisors of Navajo County that
September 8-14, 2025, is designated as “NATIONAL PATRIOTISM WEEK”. During this
event, we urge all citizens to join with the Benevolent and Protective Order of Elks in
expressing gratitude for the privilege of American Citizenship with appropriate
celebrations and observances.
PASSED, ADOPTED AND APPROVED by the Board of Supervisors on August 26, 2025.

2023 HAVA Election Security Sub-Grant Guidelines | 2 
 
 
What are the HAVA Election Security Grants? 
The Help America Vote Act  (HAVA) has been used as a vehicle for Congress to 
appropriate funds to the states intermittently over the last two decades to help specific 
parts of elections. The Consolidated Appropriations Act of 2022  (Public Law 117 -35) 
and the Consolidated Appropriations Act of 2023 (Public Law 11 7- 328), included in 
each $75 million in new HAVA funds , with Arizona’s portion set at $ 1,355,000 and a 
state match share of $271,000.  
The funds were made available to states to improve the administration of elections for 
Federal office, including to enhance technology and make election security 
improvements. This funding will provide Arizona with additional resources to secure and 
improve election administration.  State Law requires federal grant funds to also be 
appropriated by the State Legislature (see below).

https://www.justice.gov/sites/default/files/crt/legacy/2010/12/15/pl252_107.pdf

<!-- page 106 -->
2023 HAVA Election Security Sub-Grant Guidelines | 3 
 
 
 
Arizona Legislative Appropriation of HAVA Funds 
 
Laws 2023, Chapter 133, Section 83 appropriated HAVA funds totaling $5,483,500 in two 
separate line items:  
• Access voter information database  $483,500 
• Help America Vote Act projects   $5,000,000* 
*Obviously, the $5 million is more than what Congress appropriated.  Legislative staff explained that this 
was meant to appropriate any future funds Congress may approve. 
 
The state law also required the Secretary of State to submit an expenditure plan for 
review by the Joint Legislative Budget Committee (JLBC) before expending any monies in 
the Help America vote act line item.  The Secretary of State submitted a plan for 
consideration at the October 10, 2023, JLBC meeting.  The plan was amended by the 
committee and approved, allocating $3,137,900 for county distributions as follows: 
a. $2,760,800 for distribution to counties for election security grants with limitations 
(see below)  
b. $377,100 ($125,703 per year) for distribution to counties to pay their share of 
AVID costs, distributed in each of FY 2024, FY 2025, and FY 2Q26 to the 12 
smallest counties.

<!-- page 107 -->
2023 HAVA Election Security Sub-Grant Guidelines | 4 
 
 
Allowable Costs for FY24 AVID Payment Subgrants 
 
The AVID Payment Subgrant is available to reimburse the 12 smallest counties for their 
portion of the cost t o operate and maintain the Arizona Voter Information Database  for 
each of FY2024, FY2025, and FY2026 . Upon verification by the Secretary of State’s 
Office (SOS), of payment, the SOS will dist ribute the HAVA funds to the county . A 
county AVID payment is based on the percentage of the state’s total number of registered 
voters residing in that county.

Pursuant to Congressional intent under the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2020 
(Public Law 116-93), Counties may expend grant funds to do any of the following: 
 
• Replace voting equipment that only records a voter's intent electronically with 
equipment that utilizes a voter verified paper record. 
• Implement a post-election audit system that provides a high level of confidence in 
the accuracy of the final vote tally. 
• Upgrade election -related computer systems to address cyber vulnerabilities 
identified through Department of Homeland Security, or similar scans or 
assessments of, existing election systems. 
• Facilitate cybersecurity training for the state chief election official's office and  
local election officials. 
• Implement established cybersecurity best practices for election systems. 
• Fund other activities that will improve the security of elections for Federal office. 
 
Counties may also expend funds, pursuant to Section 101 of the Help America Vote

<!-- page 109 -->
2023 HAVA Election Security Sub-Grant Guidelines | 6 
 
 
Act of 2002 (P.L. 107-252) by: 
 
• Complying with the requirements under title III. 
• Improving the administration of elections for Federal office. 
• Educating voters concerning voting procedures, voting rights, and voting 
technology. 
• Training election officials, poll workers, and election volunteers. 
• Developing the State plan for requirements payments to be submitted under part 
1 of subtitle D of title II. 
• Improving, acquiring, leasing, modifying, or replacing voting systems and 
technology and methods for casting and counting votes. 
• Improving the accessibility and quantity of polling places, including providing 
physical access for individuals with disabilities, providing nonvisual access for 
individuals with visual impairments, and providing assistance to Native 
Americans, Alaska Native citizens, and to individuals with limited proficiency in 
the English language. 
• Establishing toll -free telephone hotlines that voters may use to report possible 
voting fraud and voting rights violations, to obtain general election information, 
and to access detailed automated information on their own voter registration 
status, specific polling place locations, and other relevant information. 
 
Counties may not expend funds: 
• On projects that do not meet federal HAVA or state sub-recipient guidelines. 
• On projects not approved by the SOS. 
• To pay for general operating expenses. 
 
Per JLBC requirements, these monies also shall not be expended for:  
 
• The operation or installation of ballot drop boxes.  
• The operation of voting centers.  
• Information campaigns based on the specific party registration or age of voter. 
• Election workforce programs established with a university or community college.

<!-- page 110 -->
2023 HAVA Election Security Sub-Grant Guidelines | 7 
 
 
Review Criteria 
The 2023 HAVA sub-grant applications will be evaluated based on the following criteria: 
 
• What is the project’s intent and expected result? 
• Does the project fall within at least one category of the federal HAVA expenditure 
guidelines, but not one of the uses prohibited by JLBC? 
• Does the County’s project address election technology and/or election security 
needs? If not, does it enhance and/or improve elections in general? 
• How does the County’s project benefit voters in Federal elections? 
• Was a clear and comprehensive explanation and plan for the project provided? 
 
Reporting and Monitoring Requirements 
The SOS wants to ensure the State remains compliant with all federal and state laws 
regarding the 2023 HAVA Election Security Grant award. The SOS must submit annual 
financial reports to the Election Assistance Commission (EAC) until all awarded grant 
funds are expended. Due to potential federal audits, the SOS will require County sub - 
grant recipients to provide the following: 
 
• Invoices and payment receipts for any purchases within 30 days of purchase. 
 
• Quarterly reports if funds are not fully expended within 30 days. 
o The quarterly report must provide interest earned, the amount spent and 
current balance, at minimum. 
o The quarterly report must include the category for which the funds were 
spent, to include: 
• Accessibility 
• Cyber or Physical Security 
• Election Auditing 
• Voter Education 
• Voting Equipment             
• Voting Processes               
• Voter Registration Systems           
• Other (Improve Federal Elections Administration)

<!-- page 111 -->
2023 HAVA Election Security Sub-Grant Guidelines | 8 
 
 
Sub-Grant Administration Guidelines 
Grant Guidance 
The 2023 HAVA Election Security Grant funds were funded by the federal government 
to the Arizona Secretary of State. The SOS’s Office will award sub -grants, in which sub- 
grant recipients must follow all federal HAVA laws and any state applicable laws relating 
to grants. 
 
Requesting Funds 
Counties must apply to request funds. 
 
Interest on Grant Funds 
If funds are not expended within 30 days of receipt of the funds, the funds must be 
placed into an interest -bearing account. If any interest is earned and not spent on the 
SOS approved project, the interest must be returned to the SOS. 
 
Equipment Inventory Requirements 
Sub-grant recipients must maintain inventory records of all property purchased with 
grant funds. Disposition of this property shall be in accordance with 2 CFR § 200.313. 
 
Record Retention 
Record retention is subject to 2 CFR §215.53.

Title: Cancel & Appoint Domestic Water Improvement District Election 
Submitted By: Rayleen Richards, Elections Director
Department: Elections
Motion before the Board:
Resolution Number 25-2025 canceling the November 4, 2025, Election for the Pinedale Domestic Water
Improvement District, Porter Mountain Domestic Water Improvement District and Sky-Hi Retreat
Domestic Water Improvement District and appointing and/or deeming positions vacant
Background:
48-1012. Election of trustees; vacancies
E. If only one person files or no person files a nominating petition for election to fill a position on the board of
directors of the district, the county board of supervisors, by resolution, may cancel the election for that office and
appoint the person who filed the nominating petition to fill that position. If no person files a nominating petition for
an election to fill a district board office, the county board of supervisors, by resolution, may cancel the election for
those offices and those offices are deemed vacant and shall be filled as otherwise provided by law. A person who is
appointed pursuant to this section is fully vested with the powers and duties of the office as if elected to that office.

RESOLUTION NO ______ - 25
A RESOLUTION OF THE NAVAJO COUNTY BOARD OF SUPERVISORS, CANCELLING THE ELECTIONS FOR
PINEDALE DWID; PORTER MOUNTAIN DWID; SKY-HI RETREAT DWID AND APPOINTING THE CANDIDATE(S) OR
DEEMING THE POSITION VACANT, PURSUANT TO ARS § 48-1012 (E)
WHEREAS ARS § 48-1012 (E) provides as follows with respect to Domestic Water Improvement District Board of
Directors positions:
“If only one person files or no person files a nominating petition for election to fill a position on the board of directors
of the district, the county board of supervisors, by resolution, may cancel the election for that office and appoint the person
who filed the nominating petition to fill that position. If no person files a nominating petition for an election to fill a district
board office, the county board of supervisors, by resolution, may cancel the election for those offices and those offices are
deemed vacant and shall be filled as otherwise provided by law. A person who is appointed pursuant to this section is fully
vested with the powers and duties of the office as if elected to that office.”
         and
WHEREAS A.R.S. § 16-410 (A) authorizes the Board to take such action no earlier than one hundred and five (105)
days before the election, and it is now less than one hundred and five (105) days before November 4th, 2025, Special
Election at which the Domestic Water Improvement Districts Board of Directors will be elected.
         and
WHEREAS the officer in charge of elections, has certified that the number of persons who filed nominating petitions
to fill the Domestic Water Improvement Districts Board of Directors positions is less than or equal to the number of positions
available, attached hereto and incorporated herein as “Exhibit A”;
NOW, THEREFORE BE IT RESOLVED by the Board of Supervisors as follows:
That the elections for the PINEDALE DWID; PORTER MOUNTAIN DWID; SKY-HI RETREAT DWID board member
positions, attached hereto and incorporated herein as “Exhibit A”, are hereby cancelled and appointed and/or deemed
vacant by the Navajo County Board of Supervisors.

Title: DEM PC appointments for 8/26/2025
Submitted By: Rayleen Richards, Elections Director
Department: Elections
Motion before the Board:
Appointment of Joan Audrey Harden and Debra Ann Coad as Democrat Precinct Committeemen for
their respective precincts
Background:
Appointment valid through Oct. 1, 2026. 
16-822. Precinct committeemen; eligibility; vacancy; duties; term B.  If the number of persons who file nominating petitions for
an election to fill precinct committeeman positions is less than or equal to the number of precinct committeeman positions, the
county board of supervisors may cancel the election for those positions not sooner than seventy-five days before the election and
appoint the person who filed the nominating petition to fill the position. If no person has filed a nominating petition to fill a
position, the position is deemed vacant and shall be filled as otherwise provided by law. A precinct committeeman who is
appointed pursuant to this subsection after filing a nominating petition shall be deemed an elected precinct committeeman. F. The
term of office of a precinct committeeman is two years and begins on October 1 after the primary election at which the precinct
committeeman was a candidate and continues until October 1 after the following primary election at which a precinct
committeeman is elected.

SUBJECT:  Draft Scope and Fee Proposal 
Joseph City Pedestrian Improvements project 
Post-design Services 
 
Dear Mr. Mundle: 
 
Wilson & Company, Inc., Engineers & Architects (Wilson & Company) is pleased to submit this 
proposal to provide post-design services in support of the Joseph City Pedestrian Improvements 
project during the construction phase of development. This scope and fee proposal will address 
items related to supporting the Arizona Department of Transportation (ADOT) during construction 
implementation.  
 
PROJECT LOCATION AND LIMITS 
 
Joseph City is an unincorporated community located in Navajo County, Arizona (The County). It is 
located along Interstate 40 approximately eighty miles east of Flagstaff and ten miles west of 
Holbrook. Joseph City recorded a population of 1,908 inhabitants in 2020 according to the U.S. 
Census Bureau. 
 
The Joseph City Pedestrian Improvements include concrete sidewalks, sidewalk pedestrian ramps, 
and driveways within the project limits.

<!-- page 127 -->
Navajo County Public Works  
Joseph City Pedestrian 
Improvements Project  
Post-Design Services 
pg. 2 
 
PROJECT SCOPE OF WORK 
 
A. PROJECT MANAGEMENT/MEETINGS/COORDINATION 
a. Project management/administration  
b. Meetings/communication – All meetings are assumed to be virtual using 
Microsoft Teams or similar software application unless otherwise noted (on-site). 
The following meetings are anticipated during the duration of this project: 
i. Pre-construction Meeting - 2 staff x 1 meeting  
ii. Bi-weekly construction coordination meetings – 1 staff x 12 meeting  
 
 
B. POST-DESIGN SERVICES 
 
Wilson & Company will be responsible for the post-design services described below as 
requested by ADOT and authorized by the County. 
 
1. Wilson & Company will provide bidding support to address contractor questions and 
assist ADOT in issuing addenda.  
 
2. Wilson & Company will be available to meet by phone or virtual platform, within 24 
hours of notification, to respond to questions in the field that may arise relative to the 
plans, details, or Special Provisions during construction. 
 
3. Wilson & Company will review and approve shop drawings, erection procedure plans, 
and formwork details, review proposals for substitutions or "approved alternates," assist 
the Resident Engineer in developing change orders, and provide other engineering 
services required to facilitate construction of the project. 
 
4. Wilson & Company will appoint a responsible member of the firm to be the contact 
person for all post-design services; this person shall be continually available during the 
course of construction for review and updating of design plans. 
 
5. Wilson & Company will make every reasonable effort to process any material presented 
for review in a prompt manner. 
 
6. Wilson & Company will attend coordination meetings. 
 
7. Construction modifications (e.g. Revisions) produced during construction shall be 
properly sealed and signed by a Professional Engineer, Landscape Architect, or 
Architect, registered in the State of Arizona, and shall be submitted to the PM to be 
added as part of the record set of plans. Wilson & Company will submit these properly 
sealed revisions, of the original design, in a hard copy as well as in electronic format.

<!-- page 128 -->

pg. 3 
 
C. PROFESSIONAL SERVICE FEE 
Wilson & Company will provide the above services for the lump sum fee of $18,432.24 that 
includes project-related expenses. The project will be billed on a monthly basis by percent 
complete.  
Thank you for requesting and selecting Wilson & Company to provide these services. We appreciate 
the business and the ability to continue to partner with Navajo County! 
Sincerely, 
WILSON & COMPANY

Title: Equitable Sharing Agreement and Certification
Submitted For: David Clouse, Sheriff 
Submitted By: Kimberley Willis, Sheriff's Office Finance Manager
Department: Sheriff 
Motion before the Board:
FY25 Equitable Sharing Agreement and Certification annual report
Background:
The Equitable Sharing Agreement and Certification report reflects last fiscal year's expenditures and
revenues for Federal asset forfeiture funds received through the Department of Justice and the
Department of the Treasury. Justice funds received resulted from joint investigations with the DEA, FBI,
AFT, US Postal Inspection, and Food and Drug Administration. Treasury funds resulted from joint
investigations with ICE, the IRS, Secret Service, and US Coast Guard and Customs. All funds are used
for law enforcement operations and investigations, training, equipment, joint operations, awards and
memorials, and drug awareness programs. The annual report serves as an audit of the expenditures made,
assuring they are being spent per the Equitable Sharing Guidelines.

Board of Supervisors Regular 1. s.        
Meeting Date: 08/26/2025  
Title: Personnel Policy Update - Step Progression Policy
Submitted For: Eric Scott, HR Director  Submitted By: Eric Scott, HR Director
Department: Administrative Services Division: Human Resources
Motion before the Board:
Approval of Policy Manual Update - Removal of Policy 2.13 - D
Background:
Approval of Policy Manual Update - Removal of Policy 2.13 - D (Schedule for Progression within Salary
Range)
***With the recent BOS approval, this policy section 2.13 - D needs to be removed from policy, as the
2.5% anniversary increases are being removed and not in the budget moving forward*** Section 2.13 - D
is below for reference. 
D. Schedule for Progression within Salary Range – (Revised Dec 2022)
Employees shall move to the next Step within the Range currently held every two (2) years on their
anniversary date. Employees who reach the maximum of their Range will receive a one-time increase
equivalent to the difference between the final two steps of their current Range every two (2) years on their
anniversary date.
Employees must meet the minimum performance requirements of their position to be eligible to move to
the next Step within the Range. Employees under a Performance Improvement Plan or disciplinary action
must return to normal work status and meet the minimum performance requirements of their position
before being moved to the next Step within the Range. Movement to the next Step will be made effective
from when the employee meets minimum performance requirements of the position, and will not be made
retroactive from the employee’s anniversary date.
In cases where an employee has recently received a salary increase or will soon receive a salary increase
not related to their scheduled Step Progression, the Elected Official or Department Director will
coordinate with Human Resources to determine the appropriateness and timeliness of the next scheduled
Step Increase.
The exception to this policy is the Sheriff’s Office sworn positions, who follow a separate Salary step
schedule.

Title: MOU for contracting services Woodruff

Motion before the Board:
Memorandum of Understanding between the Navajo County Library District and Woodruff Community
Library, for the purpose of contracting for services
Background:
The updates ensure compliance with basic grant eligibility requirements, simplify the roles of member
libraries and the Library District, and shorten the term length to five years. Given the rapid evolution of
technology, this adjustment allows for regular reassessment of the Library District’s functions and its
support for member libraries.

MEMORANDUM OF UNDERSTANDING BETWEEN THE NAVAJO COUNT Y LIBRARY DISTRICT 
AND 
THE WOODRUFF COMMUNITY LIBRARY 
THIS Memorandum of Understanding ("MOU") is entered into this \ 4 day of 
Av0US T 2025 between the NAVAJO COUNTY LIBRARY DISTRICT (hereafter, "District"), 
and Friends of Woodruff Community Library Executive Board. 
WHEREAS, 
A.) The Navajo County Board of Services has established a free library district 
Pursuant to A.R.S. § 48-3901, serves as the Board of Directors of the District, and has 
established member libraries through Navajo County; and 
B.) WHEREAS, the Woodruff Community Library is a duly constituted non-profit corporation in the 
State of Arizona, which operates a volunteer library in Woodruff, Arizona which has 
chosen to be part of the county free library district system, and 
C.) WHEREAS the purpose of this MOU is to ensure that all Navajo Cou ,;;,' residents have 
the full use of the District's services and facilities; and 
D.) WHEREAS the District may disperse county library district funds for any legal purpose, 
except expenditures shall not be made for any major capital improvements. 
NOW THEREFORE, the parties to this MOU agree as follows: 
I.) TERM: The term of this MOU shall begin on July 1, 2025, and be for a period of five 
years, ending on June 30, 2030. 
II.) TERMINATION OF MOU: This MOU may be terminated by either party upon 30 day 
written notice to the other party. Upon termination, all items purchased with 
District funds shall be returned to the District within thirty (30) days. This MOU may 
also be terminated in the event of a conflict of interest pursuant to the provisions of 
A.R.S. § 38-511, the terms of which are incorporated herein by reference. 
111.) COLLECTION MATERIALS: Books and other library resources should be provided for 
the interest, information and enlightenment of all residents of Navajo County. It is 
understood by both parties that all property purchases or otherwise acquired by the 
District is and shall remain the property of the Library District; it is further 
understood that employees of member libraries shall remain the sole responsibility 
of the member library and that all property purchases or otherwise acquired by 
member libraries shall remain the property of the member library. Member libraries 
shall be financially responsible for their respective libraries, including budgeting.

2025 CARE Committee Photo & Essay Contest Winner 
LACY GAYLE DEETS 
My Grandfather’s Farm 
 
When I think of a meaningful place to me, my grandfather’s farm is the ﬁrst thing that comes to mind. Located in Joseph 
City ‘The Farm’ has been the gathering place of many events. (It’s more of a ranch than a farm, but when Grandpa gives 
something a name, it sticks.) Very few days go by without a call from my grandpa asking for my help branding, moving 
cattle across the road, feeding, and so on. However, The Farm isn’t just a place where we go to work. My family has 
gone on many walks on The Farm. We’ve picnicked out in his ﬁelds where he grows his alfalfa. We’ve planted a family 
garden everyone helped weed and care for. The Farm has become a place where my family connects, and that ’s why I 
love it so much. Those memories made with my family are some of my favorites, whether it be the picnics with my mom 
when I was little or weeding the garden with my younger brother. Through work and life on The Farm my grandpa has 
taught me how to ride a horse, how to brand a calf, how to work on tractors and vehicles, how to drive a stick shift, and 
more. But most importantly? Through life on The Farm my grandfather has taught me the importance of work and 
working hard. 
Another life lesson I’ve learned from my grandfather and life on The Farm with him is the importance of community. I 
spoke of the memories made with my family on The Farm but what I didn ’t include is the amount of community members 
that are included in those few stories alone. My grandpa has taken many cub scouts in town to make smores and taught 
them how to whittle down at ‘The Farm’. When my grandfather ﬁrst taught me how to ride a horse, it was on a cattle 
roundup many community members came to help with. When my grandpa taught me how to brand a calf, again there 
were community members who came to help as well. He raises cattle, and when he raises steers, he sells them to be taken 
to the butcher. He reaches out to his family to see if they want any of the beef that comes from these steers, and he 
reaches out to the community as well. Many people in town have had the beef that comes from his farm, meaning they 
have a part of the farm with them. A lot of love, hard work, and memories go into his steers.  
‘The Farm’ isn’t just a place where my grandfather raises his livestock and their feed. It has become a place where 
community and family gather. It is a place of hard work, love, and many memories for not just my family but my 
community. The Farm is more than important to me. It is a foundational piece of my identity. No matter where I go, I will 
take The Farm with me.

<!-- page 149 -->
2025 CARE Committee Photo & Essay Contest Winner 
IAN FISH 
 A Place That Matters  
Out in the middle of rural Arizona, there's this one place that’s always been part of my life: the S Trail in Snowflake. It’s 
nothing fancy—just a quiet dirt path—but it’s where I go to clear my head, train, or just get away for a bit. As a cross-
country runner, I’ve probably run that trail more times than I can count. It’s helped shape who I am, not just as a runner, 
but as a person too. 
The S Trail isn’t super impressive or anything. It’s this 5K loop near the road to Holbrook that winds up to a big “S” on 
the hill above town. It doesn’t even have a real name, but around here, it’s the go-to spot for running. The path’s a mix of 
dirt and hills—nothing too crazy, but enough to make you work for it. And when you get to the top? The view is actually 
kind of amazing. You can see all the hills and even the mountains way out in the distance.  
One of the best things about the trail is that it’s close to home. It feels like its own little world, but it’s not far if I ever feel 
like heading out for a quick run. I’ve run it with friends, teammates, coaches—you name it. But honestly, some of my 
favorite runs have been solo. There’s just something about being out there alone, with no distractions, just the sound of 
my shoes hitting the ground. It’s like the world quiets down, and everything makes a little more sense. 
Running that trail helps me chill out when things get stressful. When life’s throwing a bunch of stuff at me, a run out 
there helps clear my mind. The rhythm of running, the wind, the open space —it’s like a reset button. It’s weird how a 
simple trail can help you think through things or just forget about them for a while.  
There was this one fall morning I still think about. I had just finished a quick loop and sat on the bench at the top of the 
hill for a while. I didn’t even think about time or school or anything—I just sat there, taking it all in. It was super quiet, 
the air was cool, and I felt completely at peace. That run wasn’t about pushing myself or hitting a goal. It was just about 
being there in the moment. 
The S Trail’s also where I learned how to push through tough days. I trained for my first marathon out there. I ’ve had 
great runs and terrible ones. But every time I lace up and hit that trail, I feel like I ’m getting a little stronger, physically 
and mentally. It’s taught me a lot about sticking with things, even when they’re hard. 
Over time, the S Trail became more than just a running spot. It’s kind of like my place to think, to grow, to figure stuff 
out. Every run out there means something, whether it’s a win or just a quiet moment to myself. It’s simple, yes, but to me, 
it’s a big deal. It’s helped shape who I am, and I know it’ll always be a part of me.

<!-- page 150 -->
2025 CARE Committee Photo & Essay Contest Winner 
SOCORRO N. PACHECO 
 When a House Becomes a Home  
 
My Nana and Tata’s house is the place in Navajo County that means the most to me. It is a home that has built 
relationships, taught me how to learn, and made me the person I am today. It's more than just a building; it's a place 
filled with laughter, sadness, family traditions, and countless memories. The smell of dinner in the kitchen, the creaking 
of the old floors, and the familiar sound of my Tata calling us out to help him feed the horses are all pieces of what made 
this house feel like a home. It has always been the heart of our family.   
For the first 5 years of my life, I spent nearly 80% of my time at my grandparents' house. I was raised by a single mom 
who didn't have access to daycare or babysitters, so my sister and I spent that time with our Nana and Tata. It was there 
that I learned to talk, to walk, and to form my strongest relationships.   
 When I was old enough to begin school, I started spending less time at their house, but every summer still belonged to 
them. As my little cousins came along, I slowly started coming around more often to spend more time with them.   
As the years passed, school got busier, and I was old enough to stay home alone. I stopped visiting as often, sometimes 
going weeks or months without a visit, until my Tata got sick. When he got sick and finally told the family, it wasn't long 
before he passed, and the family stayed at the house every day for close to a month. From that moment on, we made a 
family tradition: Sunday family dinners. Since August of 2022, I have been going over to my nana's house as often as I 
can, forming not only a closer bond but a friendship. We have become each other's person.   
I don’t just love the house; I love the people in it. Many people in the Winslow, Arizona area feel the same way. My Nana 
and Tata formed many friendships in their years and had many get-togethers at this house. At the tragic time of my Tata's 
death, the community got together to celebrate the life of their local mailman.  
This house, my Nana and Tata’s house, isn’t just a place I grew up in—it’s the foundation of who I am. It holds the roots 
of my childhood, the strength of my family, and the love that continues to grow even through loss. No matter where life 
takes me, that house will always be home.

Title: IGA between the Navajo County Sheriff's Office and White Mountain Apache Police
Department
Submitted By: Nicole Reske, Tribal Government Relations Liaison
Department: Sheriff 
Presented By: Chief Deputy Brian Swanty
Motion before the Board:
Consideration of an Intergovernmental Agreement between the Navajo County Sheriff's Office and White
Mountain Apache Police Department for a cross-jurisdictional agreement.
Background:
Since 2013, the White Mountain Apache Tribe and Navajo County Sheriffs Office (''NCSO") have
entered into cross-jurisdictional agreements that enable Navajo County Police Officers to assist White
Mountain Apache Police ("WMAPD") to enforce the Tribe's own laws within the boundaries of the Fort
Apache Indian Reservation and engage in hot pursuit of state suspects across the Reservation boundary
lines, and for WMAPD to pursue suspects across the State boundary line and to assist NCSO officers in
enforcing state law within the County, when appropriate. It has been proposed by NCSO and the Tribe
that they enter into a new three-year Law Enforcement Agreement, which requires approval of any NCSO
officers by WMAPD before certification.
Recommendation: 
Staff recommends approval.

Board of Supervisors Regular 6. a.        
Meeting Date: 08/26/2025  
Title: TXT-A 25-002 Navajo County Assisted Living Homes Administrative Procedures, Guest
Houses/Accessory Dwelling Units, and Definitions Update
Submitted By: Cody Cooper, Planning Manager
Department: Planning & Development Services 
Presented By: Cody Cooper
Motion before the Board:
PUBLIC HEARING: Consideration and adoption of Resolution Number 26-2025, approving/denying
an update to Section 1916 (assisted living home administrative procedures), Sections 502, 602, 702, and
1917 (guest houses/accessory dwelling units), and Article 23 (definitions) of the Navajo County Zoning
Ordinance.
Background:
A.R.S. § 11-820.02 requires that if counties have regulations that restrict the distance between assisted
living homes, the county shall establish an administrative procedure in which a deviation from the
separation requirement may be granted as a reasonable accomodation under the fair housing act.  As a
result of this law, Planning and Development Services Staff are recommending that the Navajo County
Zoning Ordinance be updated to ensure compliance with this law.
Additionally, during the 2025 Arizona Legislative Session, House Bill 2928 was introduced, passed, and
signed by the Governor into law.  This new law will create A.R.S. § 11-810.01 and is set to take effect on
September 26th, 2025.  This law has been passed regarding accessory dwelling units (ADUs) and
requirements for Counties regarding regulation of these dwellings.  As a summary, the legislation requires
the following: 
Counties shall adopt regulations that allow an ADU on any lot or parcel where single-family
dwellings are allowed.
At least one attached and one detached ADU as a permitted use on all parcels.
A minimum of one additional detached ADU as a permitted use on a lot or parcel that is one acre
or more in size.
There are also provisions which counties may not enforce as a result of legislation, which are summarized
in the attached Staff Report.  Of importance with the new law is that if a county fails to adopt
development regulations as required by A.R.S. § 11-810.01 by January 1st, 2026, ADUs shall be allowed
on all lots or parcels zoned for residential use in the county without limits. In updating the Zoning
Ordinance, it has become necessary to add some new definitions or revise existing definitions, which are
shown in the attached Staff Report.
As of August 14th, 2025, no comments have been received regarding these Zoning Ordinance updates. 
As of submission, this item is pending a recommendation from the Navajo County Planning and Zoning
Commission.  This recommendation will be made on Thursday, August 21st, 2025.
Recommendation:

<!-- page 163 -->
Navajo County Planning and Development Services Staff recommend approval of the proposed
amendments to the Navajo County Zoning Ordinance.
